Let me plan the proof. This is the classic "strong normalization for STLC" via logical relation (Tait's method / Girard's reducibility candidates). But we need to handle the locally nameless representation.

The key challenge here: The standard approach uses a unary logical relation indexed by type:
- `R_Bool t := strongly_normalizing t` (or value-related: t is SN).
- `R_{T1 -> T2} t := strongly_normalizing t /\ forall u, R_{T1} u -> R_{T2} (tm_app t u)`.

But for the fundamental theorem we need to handle open terms with free variables. We need a context interpretation: for a context Gamma, an environment sigma mapping atoms to terms, with `forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R_T (sigma x)`.

Actually, given locally nameless with fvar atoms, we need substitution. Let me think about what representation is easiest.

Two approaches:
1. Use `fvar`/`atom` and a substitution function on free variables, with the context interpretation `Gamma |= sigma`.
2. Use cofinite quantification / open with fresh variable, and prove the fundamental theorem via "forall L, ..." style.

Standard: Define:
```
Definition env := atom -> tm.
Definition subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...
```

Hmm, actually with locally nameless, the typical relation for the fundamental theorem is proved as:

`Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t).`

where `env_ok Gamma sigma := forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)`.

Then for T_Abs, we need `R (T1 -> T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst ... t1))`? Careful: substitution under binder — since `t1` may contain bvar 0 bound by the abs, substituting free variables in it is fine (substitution of fvars commutes with binder). So `subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1) = tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)`.

For T_Abs, we have hypothesis `forall x, x \notin L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`. We need to show `R (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1))`.

`R_arrow t` is presumably: `strongly_normalizing t /\ forall u, R_T1 u -> R_T2 (app t u)`.

We need: for all u with R_T1 u, `R_T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u)`. By definition of the relation at arrow type, `R_T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u)` should follow from the IH applied to the opened body. We need to relate `open (subst sigma t1) u` with `subst (sigma, x -> u) (open t1 (fvar x))` for fresh x.

The standard lemma: `subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst (update_env sigma x (subst? ...)) t1) u` when x is fresh w.r.t. t1... Hmm, that's a bit involved but doable.

Alternative simpler approach: since the language is CBV, and we're proving strong normalization, maybe we can do a simpler syntactic argument. Hmm.

Actually, maybe a much easier path: use a "strong normalization via reducibility" with the standard technique but avoid substitution lemmas by using a different formulation.

Alternative idea: Use hereditary strong normalization defined directly on terms without types? Let me think about whether there's a simpler measure. For simply typed lambda calculus with CBV and Bool/if, strong normalization can be proved by a syntactic translation to a well-founded measure? Hmm, that would require computing a "size" that decreases... not easy for STLC SN without logical relations or reducibility.

Another approach: Rather than doing full substitution lemmas, we can define the interpretation of terms relative to an environment and use the locally nameless "open with fvar" and cofinite quantification. The standard Software Foundations "Stlc" chapter proves type soundness this way but not SN. However the "norm" chapter of TAPL/SF (StlcProp) doesn't do SN either.

Let me think about how to structure the proof to minimize pain.

Actually, here's a thought: maybe we can avoid substitution entirely by a cleverly weak logical relation on open terms. Define the relation on terms relative to a context of free variables rather than via substitution: i.e. define `R Gamma T t` meaning t (with free vars in Gamma) is reducible. Then:
- `R Gamma Bool t := SN t` (or stronger: SN plus something).
- `R Gamma (T1->T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, locally_closed u -> R Gamma T1 u -> R Gamma T2 (app t u)`.

For the abstraction case with cofinite quantification, we need: given `forall x notin L, has_type (x|->T1; Gamma) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`, show `R Gamma (T1->T2) (abs T1 t1)`. To show the second component: for any u with R Gamma T1 u, show `R Gamma T2 (app (abs T1 t1) u)`. By SN closure under steps, `app (abs T1 t1) u --> open t1 u` (given lc abs and value u). So it suffices to show `R Gamma T2 (open t1 u)`. And we'd get that from IH at fresh x: `R (x|->T1; Gamma) T2 (open t1 (fvar x))`, plus a "rename/substitution" property: if `R (x|->T1; Gamma) T2 t` and `R Gamma T1 u`, then `R Gamma T2 t[x:=u]`? That's exactly the substitution lemma, which is hardest part.

Hmm. Alternatively, use the approach where the relation is defined on closures/environments: `R Gamma T t` where we substitute the environment in. Then `R Gamma T t` really means `R T (subst sigma t)`.

```

with
```
Fixpoint open_rec (k : nat) (u : tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => if Nat.eqb k i then u else tm_bvar i
  ...
```

and `lc_at k` with `lc_bvar : i < k`. Yes! This is PLF's Stlc (locally nameless). In this convention, indices are "absolute": in the body of an abstraction, the variable bound by that abstraction has index k where k is the number of enclosing binders; i.e., outer free-ish bvars are not shifted when going under binders. So `open_rec k u t` replaces bvar k (and only k) with u, and does not shift u because u is closed (locally closed at 0) whenever it's used in the well-formed setting. That matches the `T_Abs` rule: `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` — replaces bvar 0 in t1 with fvar x.

OK so the representation is "absolute de Bruijn indices" (a.k.a. "locally nameless with absolute indices"), where opening does not shift. Fine.

Now, this makes substitution of free variables straightforward: `subst sigma` maps fvar x to sigma x, and traverses under binders unchanged (no shifting needed since bvars are absolute and separate from fvars).

Define:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.
```

Key lemma: `subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t)` for all k, u, t. This holds by induction on t. Good, no shifting issues.

So with the "semantic" style: `R T` is a predicate on terms; env sigma satisfies Gamma if for all x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x).

Fundamental theorem: `forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)`.

Better standard approach for CBV: use the "reducibility candidates" where:

The classic approach for CBV SN: In CBV, closed well-typed terms of type Bool are either true/false or can step; SN of a well-typed closed term of type Bool — but is every SN term eventually a value or stuck? 3. `R T t` backwards closure: `t --> t'`, `R T t'` -> `R T t`.

Also the SN induction principle: since `strongly_normalizing t` is an inductive Prop with constructor `SN_intro`, we can do induction on it. But note it's a "well-founded" style: `SN_intro : (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t`. Yes, that's exactly the recursion principle we need (with the IH being on the premises, which are SN t' derivations).

Alternative standard trick: define `R T` to include "t is SN and every stuck reduct ... ". Hmm. Let me think about how the classic CBV SN proof works. 

In CBV, one usually defines:
- `R_Bool = SN∩{t | t SN}` (just SN)
- `R_{A→B} = {t | t SN and for all u ∈ R_A, t u ∈ R_B}`

and the fundamental lemma works with the "value" issue handled as follows: in the abs case, given u ∈ R_A, we must show `(λx.t) u ∈ R_B`. We know u ∈ R_A implies u ∈ SN. We proceed by induction on SN u. If u is a value, `(λx.t)u → t[u/x] ∈ R_B` (from the IH of the fundamental lemma) and then use anti-reduction. If u is not a value then since u ∈ SN and not a value... it might be stuck (e.g. `x`), but wait — in the environment semantics, u = σ(x) where x's type is A and σ respects the context. Can σ(x) be a variable? If the context maps x to A, and σ(x) must be in R_A. If we allow σ(x) itself to be a stuck term like a variable, fundamental theorem fails? Well, no: the fundamental theorem for open terms with arbitrary environments: `Gamma |-- t : T` and σ respects Gamma implies σ(t) ∈ R_T. For t = x, σ(x) ∈ R_{Gamma(x)} by assumption. Fine. But in the abs case, the argument u is a semantic argument supplied by whoever applies the abstraction; it could be a stuck term. And `(λx.t) u` doesn't step if u is stuck. So it could fail to be in R_B? Hmm, but then is `(λx.t) u` SN anyway? If u is stuck (no steps) and not a value, then `(λx.t)u` is stuck and SN. But R_B = SN ∧ application property... For B = Bool, R_Bool = SN, and stuck terms are SN. So maybe we only need SN for terms that are stuck!

Ah, here's the key: we need `(λx.t)u ∈ R_B`. By SN-induction on u:
- If u is a value: step to t[u/x] ∈ R_B, backward closure.
- If u --> u': then (λx.t)u --> (λx.t)u' and by IH on u' (which is in R_A? need forward closure R_A u u' — yes if R_A closed under forward steps) we get (λx.t)u' ∈ R_B, then backward closure.
- If u is stuck and not a value: then (λx.t)u is also stuck (no step applies: ST_AppAbs needs value u; ST_App2 needs u to step). So (λx.t)u is SN (no steps at all) but does it satisfy the arrow-type conjuncts if B is an arrow? We need: for all v ∈ R_C, ((λx.t)u) v ∈ R_D. But that term can step? `((λx.t)u) v` — for it to step via ST_App1, we need u v --> ... no wait, the outer application is `app (app (abs) u) v`. ST_App1 requires `app (abs) u --> ...`, which requires u to step or value u. Stuck. ST_App2 requires `app (abs) u` to be a value, it's not. So the whole thing is stuck, SN. But for R_D = SN it's fine; for R_D arrow, recursive argument... Hmm, this suggests that stuck terms are "harmless" — they are SN and remain SN under application to anything (since applications of stuck terms are stuck? And stuck terms are SN with no reducts.)

But R_T isn't just SN at higher types; it also needs the application property which for stuck terms holds trivially? For a stuck t, `app t u` is stuck, hence SN with no reducts, but R_D at arrow type requires more conjuncts... The recursion: R_T t for stuck t: we need forall u ∈ R_A, R_B (app t u). app t u is stuck too, so by induction on the type structure, if we can prove "stuck terms are in R_T for every T", we're done. Is that true? Let's check: define stuck t as: no step from t and t not a value (or even just no step). Claim: If t has no steps, then t ∈ R_T for all T. Proof by induction on T:
- T = Bool: R = SN, and no-steps implies SN. ✓.
- T = A→B: need SN t (yes, no steps) and forall u ∈ R_A, R_B (app t u). Now app t u: does it have steps? ST_App1 needs t --> ; ST_App2 needs t value. If t has no steps but is a value (e.g. an abstraction) — then app t u can step via ST_App2 if u steps, or via ST_AppAbs if u is a value! So "t has no steps" isn't enough for the app; we need "t is not a value" as well (stuck = not a value and no steps). If t is not a value and has no steps, then app t u is not a value (app never is) and has no steps, so it's stuck, so by IH on B, app t u ∈ R_B. ✓ (using the stronger claim: for any type T, stuck terms are in R_T; and stuck is preserved under app).

But wait, IH on B requires "app t u is stuck" i.e., app t u has no steps and is not a value. app is never a value (values are abs/true/false). ✓.

So we need the lemma: if u is SN but not a value, then either u --> u' or u is stuck. Trivially: either u has a step or not. If not, u is stuck (given not a value). Then app (abs) u is stuck, hence in R_B. 

So the induction on SN u works with the case split: SN u means all reducts are SN. We do case: value u | not value u. If not value u: case on whether exists u', u --> u'. If exists, use IH on u' with forward closure of R_A. If not, then u is stuck, so app(abs) u is stuck (need lc? no steps require... let's check ST_App1: `t1 --> t1'` premise fails; ST_AppAbs needs value u; ST_App2 needs value (abs T1 body) (true) and u --> u' (false). So no step). So app(abs)u is stuck, hence in R_B by the "stuck terms are in R_T" lemma.

Wait, but there's a subtlety with `value (tm_abs T1 body)`: v_abs requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T1 body)`. So to use ST_App2 or ST_AppAbs we need the abstraction locally closed. And "stuck" preservation: to know `app (abs) u` has no step, we need that ST_AppAbs's premise `locally_closed (tm_abs T1 body)` — if that premise fails, then there's no ST_AppAbs step regardless of value u; but ST_App2 requires `value (tm_abs T1 body)`, which fails if not lc. Hmm, but if `value u` and `lc (abs)` then there's a step. So for "no step from app(abs)u" we need: not (lc(abs) ∧ value u) and not (value(abs) ∧ u --> u'). Hmm, this is the classic issue. In the standard locally nameless setup with free variables, the environment σ maps fvars to *closed* terms (values, in the SN proof, are locally closed). `Lemma subst_open_rec : (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) -> subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t).`

Hmm, but here's an idea: in the abs case, to prove `forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app abs u)`, we can use the SN induction on u, and in the non-value case, we need to handle the possibility of no step. A generic approach: since `step` is an inductive family with concrete premises that are decidable (lc_at decidable, value decidable, and recursive step on subterms), we can do induction on t.

Hmm, wait. Is `step` on subterms enough? In ST_App1 the step is on t1 which is a subterm of app t1 t2 ✓; ST_App2 on t2 ✓; ST_If on t1 ✓. So yes, structural recursion on t works. The result term: for the decision "exists step", we need to know which reduct, which is determined. Let me write:

```coq
Fixpoint step_opt (t : tm) : option tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => None
  | tm_fvar _ => None
  | tm_true => None
  | tm_false => None
  | tm_abs _ _ => None
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match value_dec t1 with
      | left _ =>
          match t1 as t1' return (value t1' -> option tm) with
          | tm_abs T s => fun Hv =>
               match value_dec t2 with
               | left _ => Some (open s t2)
               | right _ => match step_opt t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None end
               end
          | _ => fun _ => match step_opt t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None end
          end Hv
      | right _ =>
          match lc_dec t2 with
          | left _ => match step_opt t1 with Some t1' => Some (tm_app t1' t2) | None => None end
          | right _ => None
          end
      end
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_true => match lc_dec t2, lc_dec t3 with left _, left _ => Some t2 | _, _ => None end
      | tm_false => match lc_dec t2, lc_dec t3 with left _, left _ => Some t3 | _, _ => None end
      | _ => match lc_dec t2, lc_dec t3 with
             | left _, left _ => match step_opt t1 with Some t1' => Some (tm_if t1' t2 t3) | None => None end
             | _, _ => None
             end
      end
  end.
```

Hmm wait, careful in the `tm_true` branch: if `value t1` and t1 = tm_true (not abs), then ST_App2 applies when t2 steps: value tm_true holds. ✓. But if t1 is tm_true and t2 doesn't step, no rule. ✓.

Careful in the `right _` (not value t1) branch: ST_App1 needs t1 --> t1' and lc t2. But also, if t1 is an abs but not locally closed... value(abs) fails, so we go to right branch; step_opt t1 = None (abs doesn't step), fine.

The typing/equality issues with dependent matches are annoying. Alternative: avoid `value_dec` in favor of structural match: value is exactly abs(with lc)/true/false. We can inline the lc check:
```
| tm_app t1 t2 =>
    match t1 with
    | tm_abs T s => if lc_dec (tm_abs T s) then (if value_dec t2 then Some (open s t2) else match step_opt t2 ...) else (if lc_dec t2 then match step_opt t1 ... (* but abs doesn't step *) else None)
    ...
```
Simplify: if t1 = abs with lc: If t2 is a value → ST_AppAbs. Else if t2 steps and value t1 ✓ → ST_App2. Else None. (ST_App1 impossible since abs has no step.)
If t1 = true/false: if t2 steps → ST_App2 (value t1), else None. (No ST_App1 since values don't step.)
If t1 is anything else (~ value): if lc t2 and t1 steps → ST_App1, else None.

That's clean and purely structural.

For if t1 t2 t3:
- t1 = true: if lc t2 and lc t3 → Some t2. Else None (ST_If impossible since true doesn't step).
- t1 = false: similarly Some t3.
- t1 other: if lc t2 and lc t3, then if t1 steps → Some (if t1' t2 t3) else None. If lc fails → None.

Now prove:
- `step_opt_sound : forall t t', step_opt t = Some t' -> step t t'` — by induction on t, case analysis.
- `step_opt_complete : forall t t', step t t' -> step_opt t = Some t'` — by induction on the step derivation (with the term structure). This requires that in each case the match reduces correctly and that step_opt t1 = Some t1' from the IH.

For ST_App1 with t1 --> t1' and lc t2: we must establish ~ value t1. From t1 --> t1' and value t1 would contradict `value_no_step`. And in the match, if t1 = abs with lc → value t1, contradiction. If t1 = true/false → value. So we're in the "other" branch. Then step_opt (app t1 t2) = match lc_dec t2 with left _ => match step_opt t1 with Some u => Some (app u t2) ... and step_opt t1 = Some t1' by IH, so it gives Some (app t1' t2) ✓.

For ST_App2 with value v1, t2 --> t2': if v1 = abs with lc: value_dec t2 = right (since t2 steps) → match step_opt t2 = Some t2' ✓. If v1 = true/false: Some (app v1 t2') ✓.

For ST_AppAbs: t1 = abs T s with lc, value v → Some (open s v) ✓.

For ST_IfTrue: t1=true, lc t2, lc t3 → Some t1 ✓ (with the match on lc_dec).
For ST_If: t1 --> t1', lc t2, lc t3, and t1 not true/false (since those don't step) → match step_opt t1 = Some t1' ✓.

This is doable but requires careful dependent-match handling. Coq's `lc_dec` and `value_dec` as boolean functions would make matching easier: define `lc_b : tm -> bool` and `value_b : tm -> bool`, with correctness lemmas `lc_b_true_iff`, `value_b_true_iff`. Then step_opt is a plain Fixpoint with if-then-else on booleans, which reduces cleanly with `simpl` given the boolean evaluations. That's much friendlier for rewriting.

Actually, hmm, still, proving completeness requires `if lc_b t2 then ... else ...` to reduce; we provide `lc_b t2 = true` from lc t2, then `rewrite H` etc. Reasonable.

So backward closure is easy (type-structural induction) ✓.

Now the abs case: prove ∀u ∈ R A, R B (app abs u). Do SN induction on u:
- If value u: R B (open body u) holds by the "value argument" hypothesis; backward closure via ST_AppAbs ✓ (needs lc(abs) and value u).
- If not value u: if u steps: IH gives R B (app abs u') for u --> u' (needs R A u' — forward closure of R A which is type-structural ✓), then backward closure via ST_App2 ✓ (needs value(abs)).
- If not value and no step: stuck. Then app abs u is stuck... wait, is it? ST_AppAbs needs value u (no); ST_App2 needs u --> u' (no); ST_App1 needs abs --> (no, abs is a value, no steps). So app abs u is stuck ✓. Then R B (app abs u) by stuck_R ✓.

So the only thing needing decidability is the third case. And with a decidable "steps?" test we're done. So the plan is:

1. Prove `value_dec`, `lc_at_dec` (or booleans).
2. Prove step decidability: `step_dec : forall t, {t' | step t t'} + {forall t', ~ step t t'}` — actually, we need to use it in the case `~ value u`. Let's define `step_opt` as a function and prove:
   - `step_opt_some : step_opt t = Some t' -> step t t'`
   - `step_opt_none : step_opt t = None -> forall t', ~ step t t'`
   Then decide by case on `step_opt u`.

Given step_opt_none is the crucial direction, we need it for all terms. Let me think about whether proving step_opt_sound/none is feasible. Alternatively, we could prove the decidability statement `(exists t', step t t') \/ (forall t', ~ step t t')` by induction on t directly, using the recursive decisions.

Hmm, here's a thought to reduce work: we don't need full step decidability for *all* terms; we need it for terms u that are locally closed and SN and in R A. But that doesn't simplify.

Alternatively, can we avoid needing to decide steps for u by a smarter argument in the "not value" case? Consider the possibility that u is stuck. In that case, app abs u is stuck. In Coq, to prove "no step from app abs u", we need `forall w, step (app abs u) w -> False`, which is a statement we can prove by inversion on the step derivation! Inversion on step gives the cases; ST_AppAbs requires value u — contradiction with ~ value u; ST_App1 requires abs --> t' which contradicts value_no_step; ST_App2 requires u --> u' — and here we DON'T have a contradiction, we'd need `forall u', ~ step u u'`, which is exactly the stuckness hypothesis we don't have.

So: `step (app abs u) w -> value u \/ (exists u', step u u')`. So if I prove a general lemma "if u is not a value and doesn't step, then app abs u doesn't step" — still requires the case split on stepping.

Hmm, what about this: **Define R at base type to include the property `value t \/ (exists t', step t t') \/ stuck`**? Hmm, that's trivially true (every term is one of these, constructively? No — `(exists t', step t t') \/ ~ (exists t', step t t')` needs decidability. Ugh. But wait, with `stuck` defined as `irreducible`, the trichotomy is just LEM.)

What if instead we make the induction on SN do the work: The SN induction principle says: to prove P u for all SN u, it suffices to prove, for arbitrary u, `(forall u', step u u' -> P u') -> P u`. There's no "base case" separate. So in the abs case, the goal is ∀u ∈ R A, R B (app abs u). We want to apply this principle with P u := R B (app abs u). The step is: given IH : forall u', step u u' -> R B (app abs u'), prove R B (app abs u).

Now, here's a cleaner idea: prove by **induction on the type B** (structural) that:
`forall t, lc t -> (forall t', step t t' -> R B t') -> (forall w, ...) -> R B t` — the expansion lemma, but for the specific term `app abs u` we can exploit its structure.

Specifically, for term of the form `app abs u`, let's directly prove R B (app abs u) by induction on B (structural induction on the type!), using the IH from SN induction on u as a hypothesis:

Claim: For all B, for all (abs : tm) (u : tm), lc abs -> value abs -> SN u -> (∀u', step u u' -> R B (app abs u')) -> (for value u: R B (open body u) — hmm need the body) ... 

The point: for the arrow case at type B = C→D, we need ∀v ∈ R C, R D (app (app abs u) v). We can apply the type-structural IH at D to the term `app abs u` — but the term is not literally of the form `app abs u` inside... it is! We need R D (app (app abs u) v) — the term `app (app abs u) v` is of the form `app T' v` where T' = app abs u, not of the form `app abs u'`. Hmm.

But maybe a more general expansion lemma can be proved by induction on the type *jointly with* the fact that all reducts are in R:

`R_expand : forall T t, lc t -> (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') -> R T t`

We saw the arrow case: need ∀v ∈ R A, R B (app t v). Apply IH at B (structural) to term `app t v`:
- lc ✓.
- forall w, step (app t v) w -> R B w:
  - ST_App1 (t --> t'): app t' v, R B by Hall t' (arrow) applied to v ∈ R A ✓.
  - ST_App2 (value t, v --> v'): need R B (app t v'). Here's the problematic case. Hmm. But note: v ∈ R A and v --> v' gives v' ∈ R A (forward closure). So app t v' should be in R B. Can we get it from Hall? No. From the IH at B? We'd need to know all reducts of app t v' are in R B — the same problem recursively. Hmm, but maybe we can use SN induction on v within the expansion lemma? Let's see: we could strengthen the expansion lemma to:
  
  `R_expand : forall T t, lc t -> (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') -> (forall v, R A v -> ...)`.
  
  Hmm, actually, maybe use SN induction on t itself? In the arrow case, we need ∀v ∈ R A, R B (app t v), and we know SN t and all reducts of t are in R (A→B). Do SN induction on t (the SN derivation): to show R B (app t v) for all v ∈ R A, use SN induction on... hmm, both t and v are SN. Use SN induction on v: 
  - If value v: then t is either a value or steps. Hmm, we still have the same issue in reverse.

OK here's the thing: the standard CR3 proof for CBV must handle this, and I believe the standard approach uses the fact that in CBV, a "neutral" (non-value) term applied to anything is neutral, and the expansion property is only needed for neutral terms. The terms we need to expand in our proof are of the form `app abs u` (redex unless u is not a value) — so not neutral. Hmm.

Alternatively the standard CBV approach handles the abs case by a different route. Let me look up my memory of the actual proof of "strong normalization for STLC in call-by-value":

From the "Semantics of Programming Languages" course (e.g., by Amal Ahmed or Derek Dreyer), the CBV logical relation for termination is typically:

`R_τ(t) := SN(t)` for base types... and for arrow: `R_{σ→τ}(t) := SN(t) ∧ ∀s. R_σ(s) ⇒ R_τ(t s)`.

And the proof of the fundamental lemma's lambda case: Given a semantic argument s ∈ R_σ, show (λx.t) s ∈ R_τ. Proof by induction on the *SN derivation of s*: 
- if s is a value: (λx.t)s → t[s/x] and use the substitution lemma.
- if s → s': IH on s' (needs forward closure), and (λx.t)s → (λx.t)s'.
- if s is stuck: then (λx.t)s is stuck, and stuck terms are in R_τ.

Yes! And "if s is stuck" requires knowing s has no reducts, i.e., deciding. In informal proofs, they say "since s is SN, either s is a value, or s reduces, or s is stuck" — this is a case split that's fine classically. Formalizations often use classical logic or a decidable step function.

Given the constraint "no added axioms", I'll implement decidability of stepping via a boolean/branching function. Actually, `classic` from Coq's stdlib is an axiom (Classical_Prop). The task says "must not use Admitted, added axioms, unsafe flags". Using stdlib's classical axioms may or may not be considered "added axioms" — it's risky; let's avoid.

So implement step decidability. Let's design it carefully to minimize pain.

**Decidability plan:**

I'll define boolean functions:
```coq
Fixpoint lc_at_b (k : nat) (t : tm) : bool
```
with lemma `lc_at_b_true : forall k t, lc_at_b k t = true <-> lc_at k t`.

Actually, I realize the step relation requires `locally_closed` (= lc_at 0) and `value`. Let me define:
```coq
Fixpoint lc_b (k : nat) (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => Nat.ltb i k
  | tm_fvar _ => true
  | tm_app a b => andb (lc_b k a) (lc_b k b)
  | tm_abs _ b => lc_b (S k) b
  | tm_true => true | tm_false => true
  | tm_if a b c => andb (lc_b k a) (andb (lc_b k b) (lc_b k c))
  end.
Definition locally_closed_b t := lc_b 0 t.

Definition value_b (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_abs T b => lc_b 0 (tm_abs T b)
  | tm_true => true
  | tm_false => true
  | _ => false
  end.
```
Lemmas: `lc_b_iff`, `value_b_iff`.

Then step_opt. To keep the definitions simple and avoid dependent matches, use nested matches on structure with if on booleans:

```coq
Fixpoint step_opt (t : tm) : option tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ | tm_fvar _ | tm_true | tm_false | tm_abs _ _ => None
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_abs T s =>
          if value_b t2 then Some (open s t2)
          else match step_opt t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app (tm_abs T s) t2') | None => None end
      | tm_true | tm_false =>
          match step_opt t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None end
      | _ =>
          if locally_closed_b t2 then
            match step_opt t1 with Some t1' => Some (tm_app t1' t2) | None => None
          else None
      end
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_true => if andb (locally_closed_b t2) (locally_closed_b t3) then Some t2 else None
      | tm_false => if andb (locally_closed_b t2) (locally_closed_b t3) then Some t3 else None
      | _ =>
          if andb (locally_closed_b t2) (locally_closed_b t3) then
            match step_opt t1 with Some t1' => Some (tm_if t1' t2 t3) | None => None end
          else None
      end
  end.
```

Wait, in the `tm_abs T s` branch of app: if value_b t2 is true, ST_AppAbs applies. But ST_AppAbs requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T s)` — true because `value_b t2` doesn't tell us about t1! Oops: In the branch `t1 = tm_abs T s`, I need lc of t1. If t1 is not locally closed, then `value t1` is false, so ST_App2 can't apply and ST_AppAbs can't apply; ST_App1 requires abs --> which never happens. So step_opt should return None in that case. So:

```
| tm_abs T s =>
    if locally_closed_b (tm_abs T s) then
      if value_b t2 then Some (open s t2)
      else match step_opt t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app (tm_abs T s) t2') | None => None end
    else None
```

Note: when `value_b t2` = true and lc t1 = true, ST_AppAbs gives step ✓. When lc t1 = true and t2 not a value but t2 steps: ST_App2 requires value t1 (which holds: lc abs) and t2 --> t2' ✓, giving app t1 t2 --> app t1 t2' ✓. When lc t1 = false: no step. ✓.

Hmm, but subtle: when lc t1 = true and value_b t2 = false but t2 doesn't step: no step ✓ (ST_AppAbs needs value t2; ST_App2 needs t2 step).

For `tm_true | tm_false` as t1: value t1 ✓, ST_App2 if t2 steps. ST_AppAbs needs t1 abs — no. ST_App1 needs t1 step — no. ✓.

For the `_` branch (t1 is bvar, fvar, app, if): t1 is not a value. ST_AppAbs needs t1 abs — no. ST_App2 needs value t1 — no. ST_App1 needs t1 --> t1' and lc t2 ✓. So if lc t2 then check step_opt t1, else None ✓.

For tm_if: 
- t1 = true: ST_IfTrue if lc t2 and lc t3 ✓; ST_If needs true --> (no). ✓.
- t1 = false: symmetric ✓.
- other: ST_If if t1 --> t1' and lc t2, lc t3 ✓. (ST_IfTrue requires t1 = true, no; likewise false.) ✓.

Great, step_opt looks correct. Now the correctness lemmas:

`Lemma step_opt_sound : forall t t', step_opt t = Some t' -> step t t'.`
Proof by induction on t; destruct cases; in each, check step_opt reduces and apply the right constructor using soundness of lc_b/value_b at subterms. For example:
- t = app (abs T s) t2, lc_b(abs) = true, value_b t2 = true: step_opt = Some (open s t2) and step via ST_AppAbs: needs lc(abs) (from lc_b) and value t2 (from value_b) ✓. Then t' = open s t2, done.
- t = app (abs T s) t2, lc_b(abs) = true, value_b t2 = false, step_opt t2 = Some t2': IH gives step t2 t2', and ST_App2 with value (abs T s) — requires lc(abs) ✓ — gives app (abs T s) t2 --> app (abs T s) t2' ✓.
- t = app true t2, step_opt t2 = Some t2': IH + ST_App2 with v_true ✓.
- t = app t1 t2 (t1 other), lc t2, step_opt t1 = Some t1': IH + ST_App1 ✓.
- if cases similarly.

The proof work: induction on t gives goals with the match; `simpl` will unfold step_opt. Then case analysis on the boolean tests with `destruct (value_b t2)` etc. Since step_opt is a Fixpoint on t, `simpl` reduces it for concrete head constructors. For the recursive calls, we get hypotheses. Doable.

`Lemma step_opt_complete : forall t t', step t t' -> step_opt t = Some t'.`
Proof by induction on the step derivation. Each rule:
- ST_AppAbs: t = app (abs T s) v, given lc(abs T s), value v. step_opt (app (abs T s) v) = (match (abs T s) ... = if lc_b(abs T s) then if value_b v then Some (open s v) ...). We know lc_b = true (from lc), value_b v = true. So = Some (open s v) ✓.
- ST_App1: t = app t1 t2, t1 --> t1', lc t2. step_opt (app t1 t2) with t1 match: if t1 = abs T s: value t1 → contradicts t1 --> (value_no_step). So t1 ∈ {true,false}: also values → contradiction. So t1 is "other": then lc_b t2 = true and step_opt t1 = Some t1' by IH → Some (app t1' t2) ✓. But careful: the match on t1 in step_opt needs to reduce; for t1 = tm_abs etc. we derive contradiction from value_no_step. For t1 = tm_bvar/fvar/app/if, the `_` branch gives the lc_b check ✓.
  Hmm, wait: `value t1` includes abs with lc; if t1 = abs but NOT lc, then value t1 false, and t1 --> t1'? Can an abs step? No (no rule). So contradiction anyway. We'll handle: destruct t1; for abs case, use `value_no_step` needs value (abs) which needs lc — but ST_App1's premise is `t1 --> t1'`; from step of abs we get contradiction by inversion on step.   Actually, an easier approach for completeness: prove it by `induction 1` (on the step derivation) and use `simpl`. For the ST_App1 case, the goal is `step_opt (tm_app t1 t2) = Some (tm_app t1' t2)`. Now destruct t1:
  - tm_abs T s: hypothesis `step (tm_abs T s) t1'` → this is absurd; prove via `value_no_step` if lc, or directly by inversion on step. `inversion H` on a step whose source is an abs: no constructor matches (all constructors have source app or if) → solves it. Actually `inversion` should close it since the constructors' conclusions don't unify with `tm_abs ... --> ...`. Yes, Coq's `inversion` on step with an impossible source closes the goal (no cases).
  - tm_true/tm_false: same.
  - others: step_opt (app t1 t2) reduces to `if lc_b t2 then match step_opt t1 ...`. Rewrite lc_b_iff with lc t2 → true. Rewrite IH (step_opt t1 = Some t1'). Done.
  
  Hmm, for the fvar/bvar cases, inversion on `step (tm_fvar x) t1'` is impossible ✓ (no rule for fvar). For app/if, possible, handled by general branch.
  
- ST_App2: t = app v1 t2, value v1, t2 --> t2'. step_opt (app v1 t2): destruct v1:
  - abs T s: lc_b(abs) = true (from value), value_b t2 = false (since t2 steps implies not a value — need value_no_step: value t2 -> no step; we have step t2 t2', so ~ value t2 ✓). Then match step_opt t2 = Some t2' by IH ✓ → Some (app (abs T s) t2') ✓.
  - true/false: → match step_opt t2 = Some t2' ✓.
  - others: value impossible (inversion on value).
- ST_IfTrue: t = if true t2 t3 with lc t2, lc t3: step_opt = if andb (lc_b t2) (lc_b t3) then Some t2 else None = Some t2 ✓.
- ST_IfFalse: symmetric.
- ST_If: t = if t1 t2 t3 with t1 --> t1', lc t2, lc t3. destruct t1:
  - true/false: step impossible (inversion).
  - other: step_opt = if andb ... then match step_opt t1 → Some (if t1' t2 t3) ✓ using IH.

Great, complete is provable.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.

(* 2. Free variables and substitution *)
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom := ...
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...

(* 3. lc lemmas *)
Lemma lc_at_weaken : ...
Lemma lc_at_open_fvar : forall k t x, lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) <-> lc_at (S k) t.
Lemma lc_at_open : forall k t u, lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).
Lemma lc_at_subst : forall k t sigma, lc_at k t -> (forall x, lc_at k (sigma x)) -> lc_at k (subst sigma t).
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall sigma k u t, (forall x, lc_at 0 (sigma x)) -> subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t).
Lemma subst_cons_notin : forall sigma x u t, ~ In x (fv t) -> subst (cons_env sigma x u) t = subst sigma t.

(* 4. Value lemmas *)
Lemma value_lc : value t -> locally_closed t.
Lemma value_no_step : value t -> forall t', ~ step t t'.
Lemma step_lc_fwd / step_lc_back.
Lemma open_lc : lc_at 1 t -> locally_closed u -> locally_closed (open t u). (special case)

(* 5. Decidability *)
Fixpoint lc_b ...
Fixpoint value_b ...
Definition step_opt ...
Lemma step_opt_sound, step_opt_none.

Let me now think about `R`'s definition and the mutual/structural recursion.

```coq
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t /\
      (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Coq should accept this: `R` is used at T1 and T2 which are subterms of T. Note the definition is not in a `with`; the recursive calls are on smaller types ✓. But Coq's guard checker: `Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop := match T with ... R T1 u ... end`. The recursive calls are on T1, T2 which are variables bound by the pattern match on T — structurally smaller ✓. Should be fine.

```coq
Fixpoint lc_b (k : nat) (t : tm) : bool := ...
Lemma lc_b_iff : forall k t, lc_b k t = true <-> lc_at k t.
Definition value_dec : forall t, {value t} + {~ value t}.
Proof.
  intros t. destruct t.
  - right; intro H; inversion H.  (* bvar *)
  - right; intro H; inversion H.  (* fvar *)
  - right; intro H; inversion H.  (* app *)
  - destruct (lc_b 0 (tm_abs t0 t)) eqn:E.
    + left. apply v_abs. apply lc_b_iff. exact E.
    + right. intro H. inversion H. (* H : value (tm_abs t0 t) gives lc(abs) *) 
       apply lc_b_iff in H1... 
  - left; apply v_true.
  - left; apply v_false.
  - right; intro H; inversion H.  (* if *)
Qed.
```

Now let's also handle R_expand's proof carefully:

```coq
Lemma R_expand : forall T (t : tm),
  locally_closed t ->
  ~ value t ->
  (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') ->
  R T t.
Proof.
  induction T; intros t Hlc Hnv Hstep.
  - (* Bool *) simpl. split.
    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hs. apply R_sn with (T := Ty_Bool). apply Hstep. exact Hs.
    + exact Hlc.
  - (* Arrow T1 T2 *) simpl. split; [| split].
    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hs. apply R_sn with (T := Ty_Arrow T1 T2). apply Hstep. exact Hs.
    + exact Hlc.
    + intros v Hv.
      apply IHT2.   (* IH for T2 *)
      * apply lc_app; [exact Hlc | apply R_lc with (T := T1); exact Hv].
      * intro Hvapp. inversion Hvapp.  (* app is not a value *)
      * intros w Hw. inversion Hw as [...]; subst.
          -- ST_AppAbs: t = abs T s. But Hnv : ~ value t. If t = abs T s and lc t then value t. Contradiction.
          -- ST_App1: t --> t1'. apply Hstep in H. (* R (arrow) t1' *) 
              apply H... need the application property: R T2 (app t1' v). From Hstep t1' H : R (T1→T2) t1' — destruct to get the third component and apply to v ✓.
          -- ST_App2: value t — contradicts Hnv ✓.
  Qed.
```

Wait, the ST_AppAbs case in the inversion of `step (app t v) w`: the rule is `ST_AppAbs : forall T t v, lc(abs T t) -> value v -> app (abs T t) v --> open t v`. For this to match `app t v --> w`, we need `t = tm_abs T s` for some T, s. Then Hnv : ~ value (tm_abs T s), but lc(abs T s) is given by the rule premise, so value (abs T s) holds — contradiction ✓. 

Also, `R_lc` and `R_sn` are used; they're proven by destruct T / simpl / tauto. Fine.

Careful about the induction: `induction T` in the lemma with `forall t` after T. Statement: `Lemma R_expand : forall T, forall t, lc t -> ~ value t -> (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') -> R T t.` Then `induction T; intros t Hlc Hnv Hstep`. IH: `IHT1 : forall t, ... R T1 ...`, `IHT2 : forall t, ...`. ✓.

Note: R_expand at arrow uses `R_lc T1 v` and the application property of `R (T1→T2) t1'`. Good.

Now, R_step_back: at arrow, we need app t v --> app t' v via ST_App1 with lc v. ST_App1 is: `ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, t1 --> t1' -> locally_closed t2 -> tm_app t1 t2 --> tm_app t1' t2`. ✓.

Let's now also check R_step (forward closure) at arrow: needs app t u --> app t' u via ST_App1 with lc u ✓, then IH at T2 ✓.

OK. Now the fundamental theorem. Let me write the T_Abs case in detail.

Given:
- T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2).
- IH : forall x, ~ In x L -> forall sigma, env_ok (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> R T2 (subst sigma (open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) t1)).
  (Because IH is for the typing derivation of the opened body. Note: induction on has_type gives IH as a statement about the *subderivation*: since the T_Abs constructor's recursive premise is under a binder (forall x, ~x∈L -> has_type ...), the generated IH should be `forall x, ~ In x L -> forall sigma, env_ok ... -> R T2 ...`.)
- Goal: forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R (T1 → T2) (subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1)) = R (T1→T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).

So: intros sigma Henv.
Apply R_abs with body := subst sigma t1:
- lc (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)): prove via the fresh-x trick.
- Hval : forall v, R T1 v -> value v -> R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v).

For lc: pick x fresh for L ++ fv t1. Let σ' := cons_env sigma x (tm_fvar x).
  Have Henv' : env_ok (update Gamma x T1) σ' — need to prove.
  Have IHx := IH x HxL σ' Henv' : R T2 (subst σ' (open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) t1)).
  Rewrite: subst σ' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) = open_rec 0 (subst σ' (fvar x)) (subst σ' t1) = open_rec 0 (fvar x) (subst σ t1), using subst_open_rec (needs all σ' y lc ✓ — from Henv: R (Gamma y) (σ y) → lc; and fvar x lc) and subst_cons_notin (x ∉ fv t1).
  So R T2 (open_rec 0 (fvar x) (subst σ t1)) → lc_at 0 of it → lc_at 1 (subst σ t1) via lc_at_open_fvar ✓.
  Then lc (abs T1 (subst σ t1)) = lc_at 0 (abs ...) = lc_at 1 (subst σ t1) ✓.

For Hval: given v, HvR : R T1 v, Hvval : value v.
  Pick x fresh for L ++ fv t1. Let σ'' := cons_env sigma x v.
  Henv'' : env_ok (update Gamma x T1) σ'' ✓ (for y = x: R T1 (σ'' x) = R T1 v ✓; for y ≠ x: Gamma' y = Gamma y and σ'' y = σ y ✓).
  IH x HxL σ'' Henv'' : R T2 (subst σ'' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1)).
  Rewrite to `R T2 (open_rec 0 v (subst σ t1))`: 
    subst σ'' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) = open_rec 0 (subst σ'' (fvar x)) (subst σ'' t1) = open_rec 0 v (subst σ t1), using subst_open_rec (all σ'' y lc: v lc from R T1 v ✓, σ y lc ✓) and subst_cons_notin (x ∉ fv t1) ✓.
  ✓ Gives Hval.

Great.

Now, the requirement "all σ y are locally closed" for subst_open_rec: We have Henv : env_ok Gamma sigma, so for each y, Gamma y is either None or Some T. If Some T, R T (σ y) → lc ✓. If None... then σ y can be anything! Uh oh. env_ok says nothing about variables not in the context. So σ y might not be locally closed when Gamma y = None. Then subst_open_rec fails!

Hmm. Options:
(a) Require in env_ok that all σ y are locally closed, as an additional conjunct: `env_ok Gamma sigma := (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)) /\ (forall x, locally_closed (sigma x))`. Hmm, but then for Γ = empty, any σ with all-lc terms works ✓. And in the T_Abs case, the extended env σ' = σ[x:=fvar x] or σ[x:=v]: all lc ✓ (fvar lc, v lc). ✓.
   This is easy. Let's do that.
   
   But wait: is it reasonable that σ y for y ∉ dom(Γ) can be arbitrary? We could instead set σ y = tm_true for unbound variables by construction. But adding the lc conjunct is simplest.

(b) Alternatively, define env_ok with "all lc". Yes, option (a).

Wait, but there might be another issue: subst_open_rec's proof requires `forall y, lc_at 0 (σ y)` — for the fvar case, we need `open_rec k (subst σ u) (σ y) = σ y`, which follows from lc_at 0 (σ y) via `open_rec_lc_self`. Let me prove: `Lemma open_rec_lc : forall k t u, lc_at 0 t -> open_rec k u t = t.` Hmm, careful: if t is lc_at 0, all bvars in t have index < 0 — impossible, so t has no bvars. Then open_rec does nothing ✓. But under binders, open_rec increments k; the term under a binder of a globally-lc term is still bvar-free ✓. Prove by induction on t; for abs: open_rec (S k) u body = body by IH (needs lc_at 0 body — from lc_at 0 (abs T body) = lc_at 1 body, and we need lc_at 0 body: use weakening? lc_at 1 body → does lc_at 0 body hold? No! E.g., body = bvar 0: lc_at 1 ✓ but lc_at 0 ✗. But if the whole term `abs T body` is lc_at 0 then... lc_at 0 (abs T body) means lc_at 1 body — body may contain bvar 0. So open_rec (S k) u body could substitute bvar (S k)... but if body is lc_at 1, then bvars in body are < 1, i.e., only bvar 0, which is not S k. So open_rec (S k) u body = body? Under nested binders more bvars become allowed but they're all < 1... Let's think: if lc_at 0 t, then t has no bvars at any depth? No: lc_at 0 (abs T (bvar 0)) holds (lc_abs with lc_at 1 (bvar 0) since 0 < 1 ✓). So the term contains bvar 0 under a binder. open_rec 1 u (bvar 0): 0 ≠ 1, so unchanged ✓. In general, we need: open_rec k u t = t when all bvars of t (at their relative depth) are not equal to the "opening index" — which for a globally lc term, hmm.

Let's prove the needed lemma properly:
`Lemma open_rec_lc_at : forall k t u, lc_at k t -> open_rec k u t = t`? No, that's false: t = bvar 0 with k = 1: lc_at 1 (bvar 0) ✓, open_rec 1 u (bvar 0) = bvar 0 ✓ (0 ≠ 1) — OK. t = bvar 1, k = 2: open_rec 2 u (bvar 1) = bvar 1 ✓. Hmm, is it true generally? open_rec k replaces bvar k; lc_at k means all bvars in t at depth correspond... Let's see: lc_at k (abs T s) means lc_at (S k) s, and open_rec k u (abs T s) = abs T (open_rec (S k) u s). By induction, if lc_at (S k) s then open_rec (S k) u s = s ✓. And for bvar i with lc_at k (bvar i) means i < k, so i ≠ k → unchanged ✓. For app: lc_at k (app a b) means lc_at k a and lc_at k b; open_rec k u (app a b) = app (open_rec k u a) (open_rec k u b) = app a b ✓.

So: `Lemma open_rec_lc : forall k t u, lc_at k t -> open_rec k u t = t.` — Wait, that can't be right in general: consider t = abs T (bvar 1), k = 0. lc_at 0 (abs T (bvar 1)) requires lc_at 1 (bvar 1): 1 < 1 false ✗. So not lc. OK. Consider t = abs T (app (bvar 0) (bvar 1)), k = 0: lc_at 1 of the body requires bvars < 1; bvar 1 fails ✗. Hmm, so under a binder, the allowed index is exactly the depth. So open_rec at depth k only replaces bvar k, and lc_at k means all bvars at depth d are < k+d... hmm, so a bvar at depth d has index i < k+d. When we recurse with open_rec (k+d) at depth d, we replace bvar (k+d); we need i ≠ k+d for all such bvars: i < k+d ✓ so i ≠ k+d ✓. So indeed the lemma holds: `lc_at k t -> open_rec k u t = t`. 

So the "open_rec doesn't change lc terms" lemma: `forall k t, lc_at k t -> forall u, open_rec k u t = t`. With k=0: lc_at 0 t → open_rec 0 u t = t ✓ (t has no free bvars).

So in subst_open_rec's fvar case, we need `open_rec k (subst σ u) (σ y) = σ y`, which needs lc_at k (σ y). From lc_at 0 (σ y) we get lc_at k (σ y) by weakening ✓, then open_rec_lc ✓.

Good.

Now let's also double check `subst_open_rec`'s statement and proof with the all-lc environment hypothesis. 

```coq
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall sigma k u t,
  (forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)) ->
  subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma k u t Hsig. revert k. 
  induction t; intros k; simpl.
  - (* bvar i *) destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E; simpl; auto.
    (* if i = k: LHS = subst σ u, RHS = open_rec k (subst σ u) (tm_bvar i) = (if i =? k then subst σ u else bvar i) — careful: open_rec k (subst σ u) (bvar i) uses eqb k i, and we have E : (k =? i) = true, so it gives subst σ u ✓
       if i ≠ k: LHS = bvar i, RHS = bvar i ✓ *)
  - (* fvar y *) simpl. 
    (* LHS = σ y; RHS = open_rec k (subst σ u) (σ y) = σ y by open_rec_lc + Hsig *)
  - (* app *) simpl. rewrite IHt1, IHt2. auto.
  - (* abs *) simpl. rewrite IHt. auto.   (* the recursive call is at k+1, Hsig unchanged *)
  - true/false: auto.
  - if: rewrite IHt1, IHt2, IHt3. auto.
Qed.
```
Note: induction on t with k generalized requires `revert k` before induction, and u, sigma fixed. ✓.

Hmm, in the bvar case: after `simpl`, LHS is `if k =? i then subst σ u else tm_bvar i`... wait, `open_rec k u (tm_bvar i) = if Nat.eqb k i then u else tm_bvar i`, so `subst σ (open_rec k u (bvar i))` = `subst σ (if ... then u else bvar i)`. Since subst is a Fixpoint on its second argument, it won't reduce until the if is resolved (Coq's simpl may need destruct). So destruct (Nat.eqb k i) first. RHS: `open_rec k (subst σ u) (subst σ (tm_bvar i))` = `open_rec k (subst σ u) (tm_bvar i)` = `if Nat.eqb k i then subst σ u else bvar i`. So with the same destruct, both sides match ✓.

Now `lc_at_subst`: `forall k t sigma, lc_at k t -> (forall x, lc_at k (sigma x)) -> lc_at k (subst sigma t)`.
Hmm, careful: for subst we need σ x to be lc at the appropriate level. `Lemma lc_at_subst : forall k t sigma, lc_at k t -> (forall x, lc_at k (sigma x)) -> lc_at k (subst sigma t).`
Proof by induction on t (generalizing k), or by induction on the lc_at derivation. Induction on t:
- bvar i: lc_at k (bvar i) → i < k; subst = bvar i ✓.

- app: ✓.

- true/false/if ✓.

And weakening: `Lemma lc_at_weaken : forall k1 k2 t, k1 <= k2 -> lc_at k1 t -> lc_at k2 t.` Prove by induction on the lc_at derivation, or by induction on t. Let me prove by induction on t with the hypothesis (simpler with general k1 k2):
```
induction t; intros k1 k2 Hle H; inversion H; subst; constructor; auto.
```
For abs: lc_at k1 (abs T t) → inversion gives lc_at (S k1) t; need lc_at (S k2) (abs T t) → constructor; apply IHt with (S k1); lia; auto ✓.

Similarly `lc_at_open_fvar : forall k t x, lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) <-> lc_at (S k) t.`
Proof by induction on t (with k generalized).
- bvar i: open_rec k (fvar x) (bvar i) = if k =? i then fvar x else bvar i. 
  - If i = k: LHS lc_at k (fvar x) ✓ true; RHS lc_at (S k) (bvar i) with i = k → k < S k ✓.
  - If i ≠ k: LHS lc_at k (bvar i) ↔ i < k; RHS lc_at (S k) (bvar i) ↔ i < S k. With i ≠ k, i < S k ↔ i < k ✓ (need Nat.ltb reasoning / lia).
- fvar y: open = fvar y; LHS lc_at k (fvar y) ✓; RHS lc_at (S k) (fvar y) ✓.
- app: open = app (open_rec k ... a) (open_rec k ... b); lc_at k iff both; RHS lc_at (S k) (app a b) iff both ✓ via IHs.
- abs T s: open = abs T (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) s); LHS lc_at k (abs ...) ↔ lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) x s) ↔ [IH at S k] lc_at (S (S k)) s; RHS lc_at (S k) (abs T s) ↔ lc_at (S (S k)) s ✓.
- if: ✓.

We need the k-generalized induction: `Lemma lc_at_open_fvar : forall t k x, lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) <-> lc_at (S k) t.` with `induction t; intros k x; simpl; ...`. ✓.

And `lc_at_open` (for step preservation, maybe not needed): `forall k t u, lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t)`. As discussed, by induction on t. Also R_step at arrow's application property uses lc of app t' u — from lc t' and lc u ✓.

And step_lc_back (t --> t', lc t' → lc t) for R_step_back. Let's check each rule:
- ST_AppAbs: t = app (abs T s) v with lc(abs) and value v; t' = open s v. lc t (app) needs lc(abs) ✓ and lc v (from value v) ✓ — given directly by premises, no need for lc t' ✓.
- ST_App1: t = app t1 t2, t' = app t1' t2, lc t2, t1 --> t1'. Need lc t1 from lc t' = lc (app t1' t2) → lc t1' and lc t2 — use IH on t1 --> t1' with lc t1' ✓.
- ST_App2: similar ✓.
- ST_IfTrue: t = if true t2 t3 with lc t2, lc t3; t' = t2. lc t ✓ from premises.
- ST_IfFalse ✓.
- ST_If: t = if t1 t2 t3, t' = if t1' t2 t3, lc t2, lc t3, t1 --> t1'; lc t' → lc t1'; IH ✓.
So step_lc_back is provable by induction on the step derivation ✓.

step_lc_fwd: t --> t', lc t → lc t'.
- ST_AppAbs: lc t = lc (app (abs T s) v) gives lc(abs T s) = lc_at 1 s, and lc v; t' = open s v → lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v s) by lc_at_open ✓.
- ST_App1: lc(app t1 t2) → lc t1, lc t2; IH on t1 --> t1' → lc t1'; lc t' = lc(app t1' t2) ✓.
- ST_App2 ✓.
- ST_IfTrue: lc(if true t2 t3) → lc t2 ✓.
- ST_IfFalse ✓.
- ST_If: lc t1' from IH, lc t2, lc t3 ✓.

Great.

Now, `R_expand` and the SN induction on u in the abs case, and the if-case. Let me also double check the SN induction usage in R_abs.

We need SN u from R T1 u ✓ (R_sn). We do `SN_ind'` with P u := R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u).

```coq
intros u Hu.
revert Hu.
pattern u.  (* or: *)

- intros u IH Hu.
  destruct (value_dec u) as [Hv | Hnv].

    ...

Careful with R_step_back's statement: `R_step_back : forall T t t', step t t' -> R T t' -> R T t`. So `apply R_step_back with (t' := open body u)` — wait the goal is R T2 (app abs u). Using R_step_back with t = app abs u, t' = open body u: need step (app abs u) (open body u) ✓ (ST_AppAbs) and R T2 (open body u) ✓ (Hval u Hu Hv). 

Now, where does `SN_ind'` fit? The recursive structure: `P u := R T1 u -> R T2 (app abs u)`. `SN_ind' P Hstep u (R_sn Hu)`. Then in Hstep: `intros u IH Hu`. Then the case analysis. In the ~value case, we use R_expand at T2 on (app abs u): 
```
apply R_expand.
* lc: lc_app; lc abs ✓; lc u from R_lc Hu ✓.
* ~ value (app ...) ✓ by inversion.
* intros w Hw. inversion Hw; subst.
  - ST_AppAbs case: Hv : value u — contradiction with Hnv ✓.
  - ST_App1 case: the step is (abs T1 body) --> t1', impossible: since lc(abs) → value abs → no step. Inversion should close: the hypothesis `tm_abs T1 body --> t1'` has no matching constructor... actually inversion on step with source `tm_abs ...` closes ✓. But in the inversion of `step (app (abs T1 body) u) w`, the ST_App1 case gives `t1 = tm_abs T1 body`, `w = tm_app t1' u` and hypothesis `tm_abs T1 body --> t1'` — inversion on that gives no cases ✓.
  - ST_App2 case: Hval_abs : value (tm_abs T1 body) ✓, Hstep_u : u --> t2', w = tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) t2'. Need R T2 (app abs t2'): apply IH: need step u t2' ✓ and R T1 t2' from forward closure ✓ (R_step at T1 with Hu).
```
✓.

Now, the if-case R_if with SN induction on e1:
```
Lemma R_if : forall T e1 e2 e3, R Ty_Bool e1 -> R T e2 -> R T e3 -> R T (tm_if e1 e2 e3).
Proof.
  intros T e1 e2 e3 H1 H2 H3.
  (* SN induction on e1 *)
  revert H1 H2 H3.
  apply SN_ind' with (P := fun e1 => R Ty_Bool e1 -> R T e2 -> R T e3 -> R T (tm_if e1 e2 e3)).
  ...
```
Hmm, `SN_ind' P Hstep e1 (R_sn H1)` — let me structure:
```
  intros. 
  assert (Hsn := R_sn _ _ H1).  (* SN e1 *)
  revert H1 H2 H3.
  apply (SN_ind' (fun e1 => R Ty_Bool e1 -> R T e2 -> R T e3 -> R T (tm_if e1 e2 e3))); intros e1 IH H1 H2 H3.
  destruct e1; ...
```
Wait, the order: `SN_ind' P Hstep t Hsn : P t`. If I `revert H1 H2 H3` then the goal becomes `R Bool e1 -> R T e2 -> R T e3 -> R T (if e1 e2 e3)`, and I can apply `SN_ind' P Hstep e1 Hsn`. But applying a lemma whose conclusion is `P e1` to a goal that is the same — fine.

Then for each shape of e1:
- tm_true: `apply R_step_back with (t' := e2)`. Wait: `R_step_back : forall T t t', step t t' -> R T t' -> R T t`. Goal R T (if true e2 e3). Use t := if true e2 e3, t' := e2: step via ST_IfTrue (needs lc e2, lc e3 from R_lc H2, H3) ✓, R T e2 ✓.
- tm_false: symmetric with ST_IfFalse.
- others: R_expand on (if e1 e2 e3):
  - lc: lc_if with lc e1 (R_lc H1), lc e2, lc e3 ✓.
  - ~ value: inversion.
  - intros w Hw; inversion Hw; subst.
    * ST_IfTrue: e1 = tm_true — but e1 is bvar/fvar/app/abs/if → discriminate ✓.
    * ST_IfFalse: same.
    * ST_If: t1' with e1 --> t1', w = if t1' e2 e3. Need R T: apply IH with [step e1 t1' ✓] and [R Bool t1' from R_step H1 Hstep ✓] and H2, H3 ✓.
    
  Note: For e1 = tm_abs T0 s (the "other" case), ST_If requires step (tm_abs ...) — but we're in the inversion of step (if e1 e2 e3) w; the ST_If case gives a step hypothesis on e1 which is impossible for abs; inversion closes it. Actually we're doing `inversion Hw` where Hw : step (if e1 e2 e3) w; the ST_If case gives `e1 --> t1'`, and then we apply IH which needs step e1 t1' — that's exactly given! But wait, we can't have a step from an abs. Inversion on the ST_If constructor gives us the hypothesis; we just use it (it's a hypothesis, possibly absurd, but applying IH is fine — the IH requires it, and we have it). So no issue! Actually hold on: if e1 = tm_abs T0 s, then ST_If's premise `tm_abs T0 s --> t1'` is unprovable, but in the inversion we still get it as a hypothesis and use it. Since we're in a hypothetical context, it's fine ✓.

So R_if works uniformly for all "other" shapes using the step hypothesis from ST_If ✓✓. Nice.

Wait, but there's a subtlety in the `destruct e1` when e1 is a variable bound in the lemma statement — destruct works on the term ✓.

Hmm, but e1's type is `tm` and R Bool e1 was already used for SN. After destruct, H1 : R Ty_Bool (tm_true) etc. Fine.

Now, what about **R_var/R_fvar**? Where do we need R T (fvar x)? In the T_Abs case's lc computation, we used σ' = σ[x := fvar x] and needed env_ok (update Gamma x T1) σ'. For y = x: R T1 (fvar x). So yes, we need R_fvar.

Prove: `Lemma R_fvar : forall T x, R T (tm_fvar x).` by induction on T:
- Bool: SN (fvar x) ∧ lc. SN via SN_intro: no step from fvar (inversion) ✓. lc ✓.
- Arrow T1 T2: SN ✓, lc ✓, ∀u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app (fvar x) u): use R_expand at T2 on (app (fvar x) u): lc ✓, ~value ✓, all reducts: ST_App1 needs fvar x --> (impossible, inversion); ST_App2 needs value (fvar x) (impossible); ST_AppAbs needs fvar to be abs (impossible). So vacuous ✓. So R_expand gives it ✓.
Actually simpler: prove a general lemma `R_stuck` and apply:
`Lemma R_stuck : forall T t, locally_closed t -> ~ value t -> (forall t', ~ step t t') -> R T t.` = R_expand with vacuous ✓. Then R_fvar is R_stuck with inversions, and app (fvar x) u is R_stuck with inversions. But in the arrow case of R_fvar, we need R T2 (app (fvar x) u) which is R_stuck at T2 with lc ✓ ~value ✓ no-step ✓ — no need for induction on T! Actually `R_stuck` is defined via `R_expand` at the same type, and R_expand handles the type induction internally ✓.

So:
```
Lemma R_stuck : forall T t, locally_closed t -> ~ value t -> (forall t', ~ step t t') -> R T t.
Proof. intros. apply R_expand; auto. intros t' Hs. exfalso. eauto. Qed.

Lemma no_step_fvar : forall x t', ~ step (tm_fvar x) t'.
Proof. intros x t' H; inversion H. Qed.

Lemma R_fvar : forall T x, R T (tm_fvar x).
Proof. intros. apply R_stuck. - constructor. - intro H; inversion H. - apply no_step_fvar. Qed.
```

Also, in the arrow case for R_fvar... wait, R_stuck is `R_expand` at type T which internally proves the arrows by applying R_expand at smaller types to app terms — which requires "no step from app (fvar x) u". Let's check R_expand's proof at arrow: it applies `IHT2` (R_expand at T2) to the term `app t v` with hypotheses: lc ✓, ~ value ✓, and forall w, step (app t v) w → R T2 w. In the R_fvar case, step (app (fvar x) v) w is impossible, so we need to prove `forall w, step (app (fvar x) v) w -> R T2 w` — this is vacuous by inversion ✓. But in R_expand's proof, this hypothesis is *derived* from Hall (all reducts of t) — it's an argument to the inner R_expand call, proven via inversion of step... Let me re-examine how R_expand's arrow case obtains the "all reducts of app t v" hypothesis:

```coq
apply IHT2.  (* R T2 (tm_app t v) *)
- lc ✓
- ~ value ✓
- intros w Hw. inversion Hw; subst.
  + (* ST_AppAbs case *) ... 
  + (* ST_App1 *) apply arrow-property of (Hstep t1' H) to v ✓
  + (* ST_App2 *) exfalso; apply Hnv, Hv  (* value t contradicts ~ value t *)
```
Yes, this is a proof inside R_expand, independent of the outer application ✓.

Theorem normalization : forall t T, empty ⊢ t : T -> SN t.

```coq
Fixpoint max_list (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | a :: L' => Nat.max a (max_list L')
  end.

Lemma in_max_list : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_list L.
Proof. induction L as [|a L IH]; intros x H; simpl in *. 
  - inversion H.
  - destruct H as [-> | H]; [apply Nat.le_max_l | apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list L); [apply IH; auto | apply Nat.le_max_r]].
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof. intros L. exists (S (max_list L)). intros H. pose proof (in_max_list L _ H). lia. Qed.
```
✓.

Now let's think about the exact form of the induction hypothesis in the fundamental theorem and whether `induction` on the has_type derivation works well, given the context argument Gamma varies in T_Abs (update Gamma x T1). Since the theorem statement quantifies over sigma in the conclusion, we need the IH to be `forall sigma, env_ok ... -> ...`, which requires `sigma` to be universally quantified in the goal before induction. So state:

```coq
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H. induction H; intros sigma Henv; simpl.
```
Hmm, `intros Gamma t T H` then `induction H` — but Gamma varies across cases; the IH should be general over sigma, which it is since sigma is introduced after. However, there's a subtlety: `induction H` when the goal is `forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)` with Gamma, t, T being variables from the context that get substituted by the induction. Let me write:

```coq
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H; intros sigma Henv.
```
Coq will generalize the goal over sigma? No: after `intros Gamma t T H`, the goal is `forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)`. `induction H` will try to abstract Gamma, t, T in the goal, but sigma is bound after — since sigma is bound inside the goal (not in the context), the induction will handle it correctly: the motive is `fun Gamma t T H => forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)`? Hmm, Coq's `induction H` when the goal still has products will not automatically generalize them into the motive unless they depend on the induction variables. Since `sigma` is universally quantified in the goal and its type doesn't depend on Gamma/t/T, `induction H` should keep them and give IHs of the form `forall sigma, env_ok ... -> ...` for each recursive premise. Actually, I recall that Coq's induction on a hypothesis with the goal having products over variables not in the context typically works: the tactic reintroduces them. But there's a known caveat: if the conclusion's products mention the induction variables, Coq may fail to generalize. Best practice: use `remember` or state the induction principle explicitly. 

Safer: use the `induction H` after generalizing everything:
```coq
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H; intros sigma Henv; simpl.
```
I think this works because Coq's `induction` will revert the goal's remaining products? Hmm, actually no: `induction H` requires the goal to be in a form where the conclusion depends on H's indices; the remaining leading products (forall sigma) are part of the goal. Coq's elimination will produce subgoals where the products remain, and IHs will be `forall sigma, ...` if the recursive premises are also generalized. I believe this works in practice (it's the standard pattern `induction H; intros; ...`).

Let me be careful with T_Abs where the recursive premise is `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`. The IH generated should be:
`forall x, ~ In x L -> forall sigma, env_ok (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> R T2 (subst sigma (open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) t1))`.
✓.

Now the `R` development. Let me write:

```coq
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t /\
      (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Then:
```coq
Definition env_ok (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)) /\
  (forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)).
```

Lemmas:
```coq
Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Lemma R_lc : forall T t, R T t -> locally_closed t.
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof. intros t t' Hsn Hs. inversion Hsn; subst. auto. Qed.
```
Hmm, `inversion Hsn` where Hsn : SN t gives the hypothesis `forall t', step t t' -> SN t'`; then apply to Hs ✓. Actually `inversion Hsn` should work since SN has one constructor. Let me write `destruct Hsn as [t Ht]` hmm, the constructor argument naming. `inversion Hsn; subst; auto` might be simplest. Let me write:
```coq
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof. intros t t' H. destruct H as [t0 Hst]. simpl in *. intros Hs. apply Hst. exact Hs. Qed.
```
Hmm, `destruct H as [t0 Hst]` — the constructor SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t. So destruct H yields t0 and Hst : forall t', step t0 t' -> SN t', with H : SN t and t0 = t (destruct should unify: destruct H : SN t gives t0 = t by unification of the conclusion; Coq's destruct will substitute). Let me use `inversion H; subst; auto` to be safe.

Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'.
Proof.

    + eapply SN_step; eauto.

    + eapply SN_step; eauto.

Using eapply/eauto may work. To be safe, use explicit: 
```
apply (IHT2 (tm_app t u) (tm_app t' u)); [exact (Happ u Hu) | eapply ST_App1; eauto ...].
```

Now let me re-examine R_expand's proof carefully:

```coq
Lemma R_expand : forall T t,
  locally_closed t ->
  ~ value t ->
  (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') ->
  R T t.
Proof.
  induction T; intros t Hlc Hnv Hstep.
  - (* Bool *) simpl. split.
    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hs. apply R_sn with (T := Ty_Bool). apply Hstep. exact Hs.
    + exact Hlc.
  - (* Arrow T1 T2 *) simpl. split; [| split].
    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hs. apply R_sn with (T := Ty_Arrow T1 T2). apply Hstep. exact Hs.
    + exact Hlc.
    + intros v Hv.
      apply IHT2.
      * (* lc (app t v) *) apply lc_app; [exact Hlc | apply R_lc with (T := T1); exact Hv].
      * (* ~ value (app t v) *) intro Hvapp; inversion Hvapp.
      * (* all reducts *) intros w Hw. inversion Hw; subst.
        -- (* ST_AppAbs: t = tm_abs T s *) 
           exfalso. apply Hnv. apply v_abs. assumption.   (* H : locally_closed (tm_abs T s) *)
        -- (* ST_App1: t --> t1' *) 
           apply (proj2 (proj2 (Hstep t1' H))). exact Hv.
           (* Hstep t1' H : R (T1 -> T2) t1'; its third component applied to v *)
        -- (* ST_App2: value t *) exfalso; apply Hnv; assumption.
Qed.
```

Careful with the inversion of `step (tm_app t v) w`:
- ST_AppAbs : forall T0 t0 v0, lc (abs T0 t0) -> value v0 -> tm_app (tm_abs T0 t0) v0 --> open t0 v0. Unifying `tm_app t v` with `tm_app (tm_abs T0 t0) v0` gives t = tm_abs T0 t0, v = v0, w = open t0 v0. Hypothesis Hlcabs : lc (abs T0 t0). Then value t via v_abs Hlcabs, contradiction ✓.
- ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, t1 --> t1' -> lc t2 -> tm_app t1 t2 --> tm_app t1' t2. Gives t1 = t, t2 = v, w = app t1' v, step t t1', lc v ✓.
- ST_App2 : forall v1 t2 t2', value v1 -> t2 --> t2' -> tm_app v1 t2 --> tm_app v1 t2'. Gives v1 = t, value t → contradiction ✓.

The third component extraction: `Hstep t1' H : R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t1'`. Destructuring: `destruct (Hstep t1' H) as [Hsn [Hlc' Happ']]`. Then `apply Happ'; exact Hv` ✓.

Wait, there's an issue with the type of `t` in the ST_AppAbs case: the inversion replaces t by `tm_abs T0 t0`, so `Hnv : ~ value (tm_abs T0 t0)` and we have lc (abs T0 t0); `apply Hnv; apply v_abs; assumption` ✓.

Now the SN component at Bool/Arrow uses `R_sn with (T := ...)` which requires R T t' — we have Hstep t' Hs ✓.

Hmm, but there's a circularity issue: R_expand's proof uses R_sn (which is proven by destruct T — fine, no circularity) and R_lc ✓.

Now the abs lemma:
```coq
Lemma R_abs : forall T1 T2 body,
  locally_closed (tm_abs T1 body) ->
  (forall v, R T1 v -> value v -> R T2 (open body v)) ->
  R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) (tm_abs T1 body).
Proof.
  intros T1 T2 body Hlc Hval. simpl. split; [| split].
  - (* SN *) apply SN_intro. intros t' Hs. inversion Hs.
    (* every step from an abs is impossible *)
  - exact Hlc.
  - (* application property *) intros u Hu.
    revert Hu.
    apply (SN_ind' (fun u => R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u))).
    intros u IH Hu.
    destruct (value_dec u) as [Hvu | Hnvu].
    + (* value u *) apply R_expand.
      * apply lc_app; [exact Hlc | apply R_lc with (T := T1); exact Hu].
      * intro H; inversion H.
      * intros w Hw. inversion Hw; subst.
        -- (* ST_AppAbs *) 
           (* w = open body u? The inversion gives the abstraction as (tm_abs T1 body) matching *)
           apply Hval; [exact Hu | exact Hvu].  
           Hmm, careful: the ST_AppAbs case has `lc (tm_abs T T1 body)` premise and `value v` premise. Unifying app (tm_abs T1 body) u with app (tm_abs T0 t0) v0 gives T0 = T1, t0 = body, v0 = u. And w = open body u ✓. So goal R T2 (open body u) ← Hval u Hu Hvu ✓.
        -- (* ST_App1 *) exfalso. (* step (tm_abs T1 body) t1': impossible since Hlc → value → no step *) 
           apply value_no_step in ... hmm.
        -- (* ST_App2 *) apply IH; [exact H2 (* step u t2' *) | apply R_step with (t := u); assumption].
    + (* ~ value u *) apply R_expand.
      * lc app ✓
      * ~ value ✓
      * intros w Hw. inversion Hw; subst.
        -- ST_AppAbs: value u → contradiction.
        -- ST_App1: step (abs) → impossible.
        -- ST_App2: apply IH; [step u t2' | R_step Hu H].
Qed.
```

For the ST_App1 case in both branches: we have a hypothesis `tm_abs T1 body --> t1'` — actually the rule ST_App1 gives t1 --> t1' where t1 = the abstraction. Inversion on that hypothesis directly (it's an impossible step) closes the goal. ```coq
Fixpoint lc_b (k : nat) (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => Nat.ltb i k
  | tm_fvar _ => true
  | tm_app a b => andb (lc_b k a) (lc_b k b)
  | tm_abs _ b => lc_b (S k) b
  | tm_true => true
  | tm_false => true
  | tm_if a b c => andb (lc_b k a) (andb (lc_b k b) (lc_b k c))
  end.

Lemma lc_b_iff : forall k t, lc_b k t = true <-> lc_at k t.
Proof.
  intros k t. revert k. induction t; intros k; simpl; try (split; intros; econstructor; fail).
```

SN_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop,

Lemma SN_ind' : forall (P : tm -> Prop),
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
  forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t.
Proof.
  intros P H t Hsn. induction Hsn as [t0 Hst IH].
  apply H. intros t' Hs. exact (IH t' Hs).
Qed.
```

```coq
(* ==================== ADDITIONS ==================== *)

(** ** Fresh atoms *)

Fixpoint max_atom_list (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | a :: L' => Nat.max a (max_atom_list L')
  end.

Lemma in_max_atom_list : forall L (x : atom), In x L -> x <= max_atom_list L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x H; simpl in *.
  - destruct H.
  - destruct H as [-> | H].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_atom_list L); [apply IH; assumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x : atom, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (max_atom_list L)). intros H.
  pose proof (in_max_atom_list L _ H). lia.
Qed.
```

Note: `destruct H` on `In x []` — In is defined inductively, `destruct H` closes ✓.

Next free variables:
```coq
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.
```

Substitution:
```coq
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.

Definition cons_env (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y.
```

Lemma subst_cons_notin : forall (sigma : atom -> tm) x u t,
  ~ In x (fv t) -> subst (cons_env sigma x u) t = subst sigma t.

Lemma step_lc_fwd : forall t t', step t t' -> locally_closed t -> locally_closed t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' H. induction H; intros Hlc; unfold locally_closed in *.
  - (* ST_AppAbs *) 
    destruct (lc_at_app_inv _ _ _ Hlc) as [H1 H2].
    apply (lc_at_open t 0 v).
    + apply lc_at_abs_inv in H1. exact H1.
    + exact H2.
  - (* ST_App1 *)
    destruct (lc_at_app_inv _ _ _ Hlc) as [H1 H2].
    apply lc_app.
    + apply IH. exact H1.
    + exact H2.
  - (* ST_App2 *)
    destruct (lc_at_app_inv _ _ _ Hlc) as [H1 H2].
    apply lc_app.
    + exact H1.
    + apply IH. exact H2.
  - (* ST_IfTrue *)
    destruct (lc_at_if_inv _ _ _ _ Hlc) as [[H1 H2] H3].
    exact H2.   (* t' = t1, the then-branch; hmm, naming *)
  ...
```
Careful with the ST_IfTrue rule: `ST_IfTrue : forall t1 t2, locally_closed t1 -> locally_closed t2 -> tm_if tm_true t1 t2 --> t1`. So after induction, the goal for this case is `locally_closed t1` where t1 is the then-branch, and Hlc : locally_closed (tm_if tm_true t1 t2). So destruct the if's lc: `lc_at_if_inv 0 tm_true t1 t2 Hlc : lc_at 0 tm_true /\ lc_at 0 t1 /\ lc_at 0 t2`. So I need to extract the middle ✓.

Lemma step_lc_back : forall t t', step t t' -> locally_closed t' -> locally_closed t.
Proof.
  intros t t' H. induction H; intros Hlc; unfold locally_closed in *.
  - (* ST_AppAbs: goal lc (app (abs T t) v) *)

    + apply lc_abs. 
      (* need lc_at 1 t; given premise locally_closed (tm_abs T t) from the rule ✓ *)

```
Hmm, in the induction on the step derivation, the rule premises become hypotheses in the context. For ST_AppAbs, we have `H : locally_closed (tm_abs T t)` (the premise, named as in the rule construction). Actually the names generated by `induction H` for the premises: the constructor's arguments are T, t, v, then two premises. Coq names them like `H`, `H0`, `H1`... It's fragile. Let me instead use `inversion` with named patterns or use `induction H as [T t v Hlcabs Hv | t1 t1' t2 Hstep Hlc2 | ...]` to name them explicitly! That's much more robust. Let me use explicit intro patterns for each induction.

For `step`, the constructors:
- ST_AppAbs : forall T t v, lc (abs T t) -> value v -> app (abs T t) v --> open t v
- ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, t1 --> t1' -> lc t2 -> app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2
- ST_App2 : forall v1 t2 t2', value v1 -> t2 --> t2' -> app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2'
- ST_IfTrue : forall t1 t2, lc t1 -> lc t2 -> if true t1 t2 --> t1
- ST_IfFalse : forall t1 t2, lc t1 -> lc t2 -> if false t1 t2 --> t2
- ST_If : forall t1 t1' t2 t3, t1 --> t1' -> lc t2 -> lc t3 -> if t1 t2 t3 --> if t1' t2 t3

So patterns: `[T t v Hlca Hv | t1 t1' t2 Hs Hlc2 | v1 t2 t2' Hv Hs | t1 t2 H1 H2 | t1 t2 H1 H2 | t1 t1' t2 t3 Hs H2 H3]`.

But in `step_lc_back`, the induction is over H : step t t', and the IH for recursive premises (ST_App1's t1 --> t1') will be generated. With `induction H as [...]`, the IH names are automatic (e.g., IHHs). I can refer to them by the auto-generated names, which may be like `IHstep` etc. Hmm. Alternatively, use `induction H` without patterns and rely on the default naming, or use `remember`/`dependent induction`.

Alternative: prove a general "lc of subterms" and use `inversion` on the step hypothesis in a goal-directed way:

```coq
Lemma step_lc_back : forall t t', step t t' -> locally_closed t' -> locally_closed t.
Proof.
  intros t t' H. induction H; intros Hlc.
```
with default names. Coq names the rule premises in order: for ST_AppAbs with args T, t, v and premises, the hypotheses will be `H : locally_closed (tm_abs T t)` and `H0 : value v`? But wait — `H` is already the name of the step hypothesis we're inducting on. Coq will rename to avoid clashes, e.g., `H0`, `H1`. This is fragile but I can inspect errors.

Better: use explicit naming in the induction pattern and refer to IHs by generated names — Coq generates IH names based on the hypothesis name: if I name a premise `Hs`, the IH is `IHHs`. That's predictable! In `induction H as [T t v Hlca Hv | t1 t1' t2 Hs Hlc2 | ...]`, the recursive premise named Hs generates an IH named `IHHs`. ✓ 

Similarly for has_type induction in the fundamental theorem, naming premises will give predictable IH names.

Hmm, but careful: `induction H as [...]` where H : step t t' — the pattern must list all arguments including the ones bound by the constructor, including the indices? The constructor ST_AppAbs's full type: `forall (T : ty) (t : tm) (v : tm), lc (abs T t) -> value v -> app (abs T t) v --> open t v`. When destructing `step t t'`, Coq unifies the conclusion with `step t t'`, so the pattern uses the constructor's arguments: `[T t v Hlca Hv | ...]` — but the first two arguments (t, t') are indices which are determined by unification; in the as-pattern, we list the constructor's non-index arguments? In Coq, for `induction H as [a b c H1 H2 | ...]`, the pattern lists the arguments of each constructor in order, INCLUDING the ones that get unified with indices? Actually, for inductive types with indices, the pattern count includes all constructor arguments. E.g., for `le : nat -> nat -> Prop` with constructors `le_n : forall n, n <= n` and `le_S : forall n m, n <= m -> n <= S m`, `induction H as [n | n m Hle]`. So yes, all arguments.

For `step`, the constructors' arguments include T, t, v for ST_AppAbs (the indices t, t' of step are NOT constructor arguments — they're determined by the conclusion, which mentions them). Since `step : tm -> tm -> Prop` and ST_AppAbs's conclusion is `step (app (abs T t) v) (open t v)`, the constructor's arguments are T, t, v, Hlca, Hv — the indices are computed. So the pattern is `[T t v Hlca Hv | ...]` with 5 names. Hmm, but sometimes Coq requires naming the indices too when they're not inferable. Let me just try.

```coq
(** ** Fresh atoms *)

Fixpoint max_atom_list (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | a :: L' => Nat.max a (max_atom_list L')
  end.

Lemma in_max_atom_list : forall L (x : atom), In x L -> x <= max_atom_list L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x Hin; simpl in *.
  - destruct Hin.
  - destruct Hin as [Heq | Hin].
    + subst. apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_atom_list L).
      * apply IH. exact Hin.
      * apply Nat.le_max_r.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x : atom, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (max_atom_list L)).
  intros Hin. pose proof (in_max_atom_list L _ Hin). lia.
Qed.

(** ** Free variables of a term *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.

(** ** Substitution for free variables *)

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.

Definition cons_env (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y.

Lemma cons_env_same : forall sigma x u, cons_env sigma x u x = u.
Proof. intros. unfold cons_env. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma cons_env_other : forall sigma x u y, x <> y -> cons_env sigma x u y = sigma y.
Proof.
  intros sigma x u y Hneq. unfold cons_env.
  destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E; auto. exfalso. apply Hneq. apply Nat.eqb_eq. exact E.
Qed.
```
