```coq
Lemma lc_at_weaken : forall (t : tm) k1 k2, k1 <= k2 -> lc_at k1 t -> lc_at k2 t.
Proof.
  induction t; intros k1 k2 Hle H; inversion H; subst.
  - apply lc_bvar. lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; eauto.
  - apply lc_abs. apply IHt with (k1 := S k1); [lia | assumption].
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
  - apply lc_if; eauto.
Qed.
```
Hmm, in the app case, `eauto` needs to use IHt1 and IHt2 with Hle; the goals are `lc_at k2 t1` and `lc_at k2 t2`; eauto uses IHt1 : forall k1 k2, k1 <= k2 -> lc_at k1 t1 -> lc_at k2 t1 ✓ with Hle and H1 ✓. Should work.

`lc_at_open_fvar`:
```coq
Lemma lc_at_open_fvar : forall (t : tm) k x,
  lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) <-> lc_at (S k) t.
Proof.
  induction t; intros k x; simpl.
  - (* bvar *) destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
    + split; intros H.
      * apply lc_bvar. apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. lia.
      * apply lc_fvar.
    + split; intros H.
      * inversion H; subst. apply lc_bvar. assumption.
      * apply lc_bvar. assert (i <> k). { intro Heq. rewrite Heq in E. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl in E. discriminate. } 
        inversion H; subst. lia.
  - (* fvar *) split; intros H; apply lc_fvar.
  - (* app *) split; intros H; inversion H; subst; apply lc_app; 
      [ apply (proj1 (IHt1 k x)); assumption | apply (proj1 (IHt2 k x)); assumption
      | apply (proj2 (IHt1 k x)); assumption | apply (proj2 (IHt2 k x)); assumption ].
  - (* abs *) split; intros H; inversion H; subst; apply lc_abs;
      [ apply (proj1 (IHt (S k) x)); assumption | apply (proj2 (IHt (S k) x)); assumption ].
  - (* true *) split; intros H; apply lc_true.
  - (* false *) split; intros H; apply lc_false.
  - (* if *) split; intros H; inversion H; subst; apply lc_if; 
      [ apply (proj1 (IHt1 k x)); assumption | apply (proj1 (IHt2 k x)); assumption | apply (proj1 (IHt3 k x)); assumption
      | apply (proj2 (IHt1 k x)); assumption | apply (proj2 (IHt2 k x)); assumption | apply (proj2 (IHt3 k x)); assumption ].
Qed.
```
Hmm, the app case: after `inversion H; subst`, in the forward direction H : lc_at k (tm_app (open_rec k (fvar x) t1) (open_rec k (fvar x) t2)), inversion gives lc_at k of each; goal lc_at (S k) (tm_app t1 t2) → lc_app → two goals ✓. Using the bullets with explicit proj1/proj2 might be mixed up in one tactic line; let me write it with `;` and appropriate order. Since `apply lc_app` splits into 2 goals, and the subsequent `[ ... ]` bracket list must have 2 entries. Let me restructure: for the app case, write

```
  - (* app *)
    split.
    + intros H. inversion H; subst. apply lc_app.
      * apply (proj1 (IHt1 k x)); assumption.
      * apply (proj1 (IHt2 k x)); assumption.
    + intros H. inversion H; subst. apply lc_app.
      * apply (proj2 (IHt1 k x)); assumption.
      * apply (proj2 (IHt2 k x)); assumption.
```
Clearer ✓.

Wait, careful: in the forward direction of the app case, the goal after `split; intros H` is `lc_at (S k) (tm_app t1 t2)`. Inversion of H gives `H2 : lc_at k (open_rec k (fvar x) t1)` and `H3 : lc_at k (open_rec k (fvar x) t2)` — assuming the terms are syntactically `tm_app (open_rec ... t1) (open_rec ... t2)`. But after `simpl` at the start, the LHS is literally `lc_at k (tm_app (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t1) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t2))`? `lc_at_open`:
```coq
Lemma lc_at_open : forall (t : tm) k u,
  lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).
Proof.
  induction t; intros k u H Hu; simpl; inversion H; subst.
  - (* bvar *) destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst. exact Hu.
    + apply lc_bvar. assert (i <> k) by (intro Heq; rewrite Heq in E; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl in E; discriminate). lia.
  - (* fvar *) apply lc_fvar.
  - (* app *) apply lc_app; eauto.
  - (* abs *) apply lc_abs. apply IHt with (k := S k). 
    + assumption.
    + apply lc_at_weaken with (k1 := k); [lia | assumption].
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
  - apply lc_if; eauto.
Qed.
```
Hmm, for the abs case, H after inversion is `lc_at (S (S k)) t`? Let's see: H : lc_at (S k) (tm_abs T t), inversion gives `lc_at (S (S k)) t` ✓. Goal after `apply lc_abs`: `lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u t)`. Apply IHt with k := S k: needs `lc_at (S (S k)) t` ✓ and `lc_at (S k) u` from weakening ✓.

`open_rec_lc`:
```coq
Lemma open_rec_lc : forall (t : tm) k u, lc_at k t -> open_rec k u t = t.
Proof.
  induction t; intros k u H; simpl; inversion H; subst; auto.
  - (* bvar *) destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E; auto. apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. exfalso. lia.
  - (* app *) rewrite IHt1; auto. rewrite IHt2; auto.
  - (* abs *) rewrite IHt; auto.
  - (* if *) rewrite IHt1; auto. rewrite IHt2; auto. rewrite IHt3; auto.
Qed.
```
Careful: `inversion H; subst; auto` before destructing — for the bvar case the goal is `(if k =? i then u else tm_bvar i) = tm_bvar i`. `destruct ... eqn:E; auto` leaves the true case: `u = tm_bvar i` with i < k and i = k → contradiction → `exfalso; lia` ✓. Hmm, `auto` after destruct might close the false case ✓ and for other cases the goals are `tm_fvar x = tm_fvar x` etc. ✓, app: `tm_app (open_rec ...) (...) = tm_app t1 t2` needs the IHs ✓.

But there's an issue: `inversion H; subst; auto` — the inversion introduces hypotheses; the subsequent bullets refer to goals. For the app case the goal after inversion is `tm_app (open_rec k u t1) (open_rec k u t2) = tm_app t1 t2`. `auto` doesn't use IHt1/IHt2 unless they're in the context with the right form. They are: IHt1 : forall k u, lc_at k t1 -> open_rec k u t1 = t1 ✓ and we have the lc hypothesis. `auto` might use them via `eauto`-style? `auto` does use hypotheses with `forall` by applying them, yes (auto uses intros/apply/exact and simple tactics). Hmm, `auto` with a hypothesis `forall k u, ...` — it would need to apply it and solve the premises; auto can do that ✓ (auto uses `apply` with hypotheses). I think it works. If not, write explicitly.

Lemma lc_at_app_inv : forall k a b, lc_at k (tm_app a b) -> lc_at k a /\ lc_at k b.
Proof. intros k a b H; inversion H; auto. Qed.
Lemma lc_at_abs_inv : forall k T b, lc_at k (tm_abs T b) -> lc_at (S k) b.

Lemma lc_at_if_inv : forall k a b c, lc_at k (tm_if a b c) -> lc_at k a /\ lc_at k b /\ lc_at k c.

The IH names: with `induction H`, for constructor T_Var the hypothesis `Gamma x = Some T` is named H or similar... The IHs for T_App would be named `IHhas_type1` and `IHhas_type2` if the premises are named `has_type` and `has_type0`. This is fragile. Let me name the premises explicitly with an as-pattern:

The `has_type` constructors:
- T_Var : forall Gamma x T, Gamma x = Some T -> has_type Gamma (tm_fvar x) T
- T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2)
- T_App : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2, has_type Gamma t1 (T1 -> T2) -> has_type Gamma t2 T1 -> has_type Gamma (tm_app t1 t2) T2
- T_True : forall Gamma, has_type Gamma tm_true Ty_Bool
- T_False : forall Gamma, has_type Gamma tm_false Ty_Bool
- T_If : forall Gamma t1 t2 t3 T, has_type Gamma t1 Ty_Bool -> has_type Gamma t2 T -> has_type Gamma t3 T -> has_type Gamma (tm_if t1 t2 t3) T

As-pattern: `induction H as [Gamma x T Hvar | L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hbody | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 H1 H2 | Gamma | Gamma | Gamma t1 t2 t3 T H1 H2 H3]`.

Then IHs: `IHHvar`, `IHHbody`, `IHH1`, `IHH2`, `IHH1`, `IHH2`, `IHH3` (names based on premise names) ✓.

Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t /\
      (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.

Definition env_ok (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)) /\
  (forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)).

Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof. intros T t H. destruct T; simpl in H; tauto. Qed.

Lemma R_lc : forall T t, R T t -> locally_closed t.
Proof. intros T t H. destruct T; simpl in H; tauto. Qed.

Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' Hsn Hs. inversion Hsn; subst. auto.
Qed.

Lemma SN_ind' : forall (P : tm -> Prop),
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
  forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t.
Proof.
  intros P Hstep t Hsn. induction Hsn. apply Hstep. intros t' Hs. assumption.
Qed.

Qed.

Now the fundamental theorem. Let me write it.

```coq
Theorem fundamental : forall (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty),
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H as [Gamma0 x S Hvar
                | L Gamma0 T1 T2 t1 Hbody
                | Gamma0 t1 t2 T1 T2 Happ Harg
                | Gamma0
                | Gamma0
                | Gamma0 t1 t2 t3 S Hc Hthen Helse];
  intros sigma Henv.
  ...
```
Hmm, for the typing induction, recursive premises: T_App's two premises are recursive; T_If's three. Per the pattern rule, recursive args consume 2 names each. So:
- T_Var: args Gamma x T (indices?) and Hvar → `Gamma0 x S Hvar`.

**Best robust approach**: use the induction principle explicitly with a motive:

```coq
refine (has_type_ind
  (fun Gamma t T _ => forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t))
  _ _ _ _ _ _ ).
```
Then each goal is a separate case with the hypotheses named as in the constructor, and I can `intros` them in the exact order I choose! That's fully controllable.

`has_type_ind`'s statement:
```
has_type_ind : forall P : context -> tm -> ty -> Prop,
  (forall Gamma x T, Gamma x = Some T -> P Gamma (tm_fvar x) T) ->
  (forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1,
      (forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) ->
      P Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2)) ->
  (forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2, P Gamma t1 (T1 -> T2) -> P Gamma t2 T1 -> P Gamma (tm_app t1 t2) T2) ->
  (forall Gamma, P Gamma tm_true Ty_Bool) ->
  (forall Gamma, P Gamma tm_false Ty_Bool) ->
  (forall Gamma t1 t2 t3 T, P Gamma t1 Ty_Bool -> P Gamma t2 T -> P Gamma t3 T -> P Gamma (tm_if t1 t2 t3) T) ->
  forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> P Gamma t T.
```
Note: for the T_Abs case, the premise is `forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` — i.e., the IH is *already* the motive applied (not requiring an extra induction). 

So with this, I can `refine (has_type_ind (fun Gamma t T _ => forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)) _ _ _ _ _ _ H)` hmm, the order: the recursor takes the case functions then Gamma t T then the proof. Let me apply it as a function to H at the end.

Then each case goal has explicit hypotheses I can name with intros. 

Let me write:

```coq
Theorem fundamental : forall (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty),
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  refine (has_type_ind (fun Gamma t T _ => forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)) _ _ _ _ _ _ Gamma t T H).
  - (* T_Var *) intros Gamma0 x S Hvar sigma [Henv Hlcs]. simpl. apply (Henv x S Hvar).
  - (* T_Abs *) intros L Gamma0 T1 T2 t1 Hbody sigma [Henv Hlcs]. simpl.
    ...
  - (* T_App *) intros Gamma0 t1 t2 T1 T2 Hh IHapp Harg IHarg sigma [Henv Hlcs]. simpl. ...
  - (* T_True *) intros Gamma0 sigma [Henv Hlcs]. simpl. split; ...
  - (* T_False *) ...
  - (* T_If *) intros Gamma0 t1 t2 t3 S Hc IHc Hthen IHthen Helse IHelse sigma [Henv Hlcs]. simpl. ...
Qed.
```

Wait, careful with the shape of the case functions: For T_Abs, the case argument is:
`forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) -> P Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2)`.
Since P Gamma t T = `forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)`, the P at the recursive occurrence expands to `forall sigma, env_ok (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> R T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x)))`.

When I `intros L Gamma0 T1 T2 t1 Hbody sigma [Henv Hlcs]`, the goal is `R (T1→T2) (subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1))` ✓ and Hbody : forall x, ~ In x L -> forall sigma, env_ok (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> R T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x))) ✓ — exactly what I used before as IHbody ✓.

This gives full control over names! Much better than induction. Coq's `has_type_ind` should be automatically generated (the inductive is not in a section with local definitions... it's inside a Module, but the recursor is generated).

Note: The order of the case arguments in has_type_ind matches the constructor order: T_Var, T_Abs, T_App, T_True, T_False, T_If ✓.
